### Arnold press

`arnold_press` · 2 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Dumbbell/kettlebell press that starts palms-facing with the elbows in front of the body and rotates to palms-forward at the top, adding a horizontal-abduction and external-rotation sweep to the vertical press and extending the anterior-deltoid range of motion at the bottom.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Prime mover; the extra bottom-range flexion from the palms-in start lengthens the anterior deltoid. An unverified EMG comparison reports higher anterior and medial deltoid activity than the standard dumbbell press. |
| delt_side | 0.5 | Synergist; the rotate-out phase adds abduction work (kept from current). |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Elbow extension with the shoulder elevated; medial-head bias per Kholinne 2018. |
| traps_upper | 0.5 | Scapular upward rotation/elevation as in any overhead press. |
| chest | 0.25 {chest_upper 0.8, chest_mid 0.2} | Palms-in, elbows-forward start puts the clavicular pec in a flexion/horizontal-adduction role in the bottom range (front-press pec finding of Coratella 2022 applies at least as strongly). |

Exercises: Arnold Dumbbell Press, Kettlebell Arnold Press.

Evidence:

- [emg, unverified] Arnold dumbbell press produced higher anterior (1346 vs 1043 uV) and medial deltoid sEMG than the overhead dumbbell press in 10 men. — Raizada S, Bagchi A. Comparison among the EMG Activity of the Anterior Deltoid and Medial Deltoid During Two Variations of Dumbbell Shoulder Press Exercise. Indian J Public Health Res Dev. 2017;8(4).
- [emg, verified] Front-of-head pressing with a neutral handgrip favours pectoralis major excitation compared with behind-neck pressing. — Coratella G, Tornatore G, Longo S, Esposito F, Ce E. Front vs Back and Barbell vs Machine Overhead Press: An Electromyographic Analysis and Implications For Resistance Training. Front Physiol. 2022;13:825880. PMID 35936912.
- [emg, verified] Anterior deltoid is the dominant deltoid head in dumbbell shoulder pressing; medial deltoid is a strong secondary. — Campos YAC, et al. Different Shoulder Exercises Affect the Activation of Deltoid Portions in Resistance-Trained Individuals. J Hum Kinet. 2020;75:5-14. PMID 33312291.
- [emg, verified] Medial head of triceps dominates elbow extension at high shoulder elevation. — Kholinne E, et al. The different role of each head of the triceps brachii muscle in elbow extension. Acta Orthop Traumatol Turc. 2018;52(3):201-205. PMID 29503079.

Notes: Mapping ends up identical to the bilateral front press; kept as its own family because the only direct EMG comparison (Raizada & Bagchi 2017) could not be opened in PubMed and users expect the Arnold press to be recognised. If the table needs fewer rows, merge into overhead_press with no override.

### Behind-the-neck and Bradford press

`behind_neck_press` · 3 exercises · confidence high, unverified

Barbell press with the bar travelling behind the head (or, in the Bradford/Rocky press, alternating front-to-back over the head without lockout). The humerus is held in the frontal plane in full external rotation, shifting the moment demand from the anterior to the middle and posterior deltoid and removing the pec's flexion contribution.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Still the largest mover; Coratella 2022 found anterior deltoid equal or higher in back vs front barbell press (higher in the descending phase, ES 7.7). |
| delt_side | 0.75 | RAISED from 0.5: medial deltoid RMS was much greater in back than front barbell press (ES 3.68 ascending, 4.56 descending) because the frontal-plane arm path is nearly pure abduction. |
| delt_rear | 0.25 | ADDED: posterior deltoid was far more excited in back than front press (ES 9.0 ascending, 8.65 descending); the externally rotated, frontal-plane humerus recruits it as an abductor/external rotator. Still a minor contributor in absolute terms. |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Elbow extension to lockout; medial-head bias per Kholinne 2018. |
| traps_upper | 0.5 | Upper trapezius active in all overhead press variants with no front/back difference (Coratella 2022). |

Exercises: Bradford/Rocky Presses, Standing Barbell Press Behind Neck, Standing Bradford Press.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Bradford/Rocky Presses**: delt_front 1; delt_side 0.75; delt_rear 0.25; triceps 0.25 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6}; traps_upper 0.5 — Bar passes over the head at partial elbow extension and is never locked out, so the triceps see only the mid-range; constant-tension deltoid work is the point of the exercise. Triceps reduced to 0.25 (anatomical/consensus).
- **Standing Bradford Press**: delt_front 1; delt_side 0.75; delt_rear 0.25; triceps 0.25 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6}; traps_upper 0.5 — Same as Bradford_Rocky_Presses: no lockout, triceps 0.25.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] Behind-neck barbell press produces greater medial (ES 3.68) and posterior (ES 9.0) deltoid excitation than front press, while front press favours pectoralis major (ES 3.11); upper trapezius did not differ. — Coratella G, Tornatore G, Longo S, Esposito F, Ce E. Front vs Back and Barbell vs Machine Overhead Press: An Electromyographic Analysis and Implications For Resistance Training. Front Physiol. 2022;13:825880. PMID 35936912.
- [anatomical, likely] Behind-head and in-front pressing differ in shoulder ROM and spine posture; both are tolerable for lifters with normal mobility. Describes the frontal-plane arm path that drives the middle/posterior deltoid emphasis. — McKean MR, Burkett BJ. Overhead shoulder press - In-front of the head or behind the head? J Sport Health Sci. 2015;4(3):250-257. doi:10.1016/j.jshs.2013.11.007.
- [emg, verified] Medial head of triceps dominates elbow extension at 90-180 degrees of shoulder elevation. — Kholinne E, et al. The different role of each head of the triceps brachii muscle in elbow extension. Acta Orthop Traumatol Turc. 2018;52(3):201-205. PMID 29503079.

Notes: No chest credit (front-press pec contribution disappears behind the neck). External-rotation demand is high; per the brief's proxy rule this is expressed through delt_rear rather than a rotator-cuff group. Bradford presses never lock out, so triceps credit is halved for them.

### Push press

`push_press` · 2 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Overhead press initiated by a shallow dip-and-drive of the legs (quarter-squat depth, triple extension of hips, knees and ankles) that launches the implement, with the arms finishing the lockout and controlling the eccentric. Kettlebell single- and double-arm versions.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Prime mover for the arm phase and the loaded eccentric; heavier loads than the strict press offset the leg assist (consensus; Soriano 2019). |
| delt_side | 0.5 | Synergist as in the strict press. |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Finishes the lockout; medial-head bias per Kholinne 2018. |
| traps_upper | 0.5 | Upward rotation/elevation at lockout plus a shrug component in the drive. |
| quads | 0.5 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9} | Kept at 0.5: the dip-drive produces jump-squat-level peak and mean power (Lake 2014), so the knee extensors do real work, but only through a quarter-squat range. Vasti-dominant as in the squat anchor. |
| glutes | 0.25 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1} | ADDED at minor credit: hip extension is part of the triple extension (Soriano 2019) but from a shallow, near-upright position where glute demand is small. |
| calves | 0.25 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3} | REDUCED from 0.5: ankle plantarflexion is a brief terminal pop of the drive, not a loaded through-range contraction; standing (knee-extended) so gastrocnemius-biased as in the standing calf raise anchor. |

Exercises: Double Kettlebell Push Press, One-Arm Kettlebell Push Press.

Per-exercise differences:

- **One-Arm Kettlebell Push Press**: delt_front 1; delt_side 0.5; triceps 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6}; traps_upper 0.5; quads 0.5 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9}; glutes 0.25 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1}; calves 0.25 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3}; obliques 0.25 — Unilateral load adds an anti-lateral-flexion demand (Saeterbakken & Fimland 2012: external oblique 68% higher in standing unilateral vs bilateral press); obliques 0.25.

Evidence:

- [anatomical, verified] Push press peak and mean power are maximised at 65-75% 1RM and are comparable to (mean power ~9.5% greater than) the jump squat, showing a substantial lower-body contribution. — Lake JP, Mundy PD, Comfort P. Power and impulse applied during push press exercise. J Strength Cond Res. 2014;28(9):2552-2559. PMID 24584046.
- [consensus, verified] Weightlifting overhead pressing derivatives (push press, push jerk, split jerk) rely on an impulsive triple extension of hips, knees and ankles from a shallow dip; longitudinal hypertrophy data are lacking. — Soriano MA, Suchomel TJ, Comfort P. Weightlifting Overhead Pressing Derivatives: A Review of the Literature. Sports Med. 2019;49(6):867-885. PMID 30924081.
- [anatomical, verified] Kinetic/kinematic profile of push press vs push jerk vs split jerk; the push press has the longest, most muscularly driven arm phase of the three. — Soriano MA, Jimenez-Ormeno E, Lake JP, et al. Kinetics and Kinematics of the Push Press, Push Jerk, and Split Jerk. J Strength Cond Res. 2024;38(8):1359-1365. PMID 39072653.
- [emg, verified] Deltoid activation pattern of the standing overhead press (anterior > medial) carries over to the arm phase of the push press. — Saeterbakken AH, Fimland MS. Effects of body position and loading modality on muscle activity and strength in shoulder presses. J Strength Cond Res. 2013;27(7):1824-1831. PMID 23096062.

Notes: No direct EMG of the push press in PubMed; leg credits rest on kinetics plus anatomy. No hamstring credit (quarter-squat, knee-dominant dip) and no abs credit (isometric bracing).

### Clean and press

`clean_and_press` · 1 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Barbell power clean from the floor (hip-dominant pull, triple extension, shrug, rack) followed by a strict overhead press. Two movements in one rep: a posterior-chain/quad pull and a vertical press.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Prime mover of the press half; the exercise is logged under shoulder pressing. |
| delt_side | 0.5 | Press synergist. |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Press lockout; medial-head bias overhead (Kholinne 2018). |
| chest | 0.25 {chest_upper 0.8, chest_mid 0.2} | Front-press clavicular pec contribution (Coratella 2022). |
| traps_upper | 0.75 | RAISED from 0.5: trapezius is a dominant muscle in the hang power clean (Geisler 2023) via the second-pull shrug, and it works again in the press lockout. |
| upper_back | 0.5 {traps_mid_lower 0.5, rhomboids 0.5} | Kept: scapular retraction/fixation during the pull and rack. |
| erectors | 0.5 | Kept: erector spinae is highly active through the first and second pull (Geisler 2023), consistent with the RDL anchor's 0.5. |
| glutes | 0.5 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1} | Kept at 0.5: gluteus maximus drives hip extension in the pull (Geisler 2023). Region split aligned to the squat anchor (.9/.1) instead of current .8/.2. |
| hamstrings | 0.5 {hamstrings_hip_ext 1} | Kept at 0.5 but region split CHANGED from .8/.2 to 1.0/0: the pull is hip extension with the knee extending, which the short head (knee flexor only) cannot contribute to, matching the RDL anchor. |
| quads | 0.5 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9} | Kept: vastus lateralis strongly active in the first pull and catch (Geisler 2023). |
| calves | 0.25 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3} | REDUCED from 0.5: plantarflexion is a brief terminal component of the second pull. |

Exercises: Clean and Press.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] During the hang power clean, vastus lateralis, gluteus maximus, erector spinae and trapezius are the predominant active muscles (with rectus abdominis as stabiliser), with a similar pattern to the clean pull at 70-90% 1RM. — Geisler S, Havers T, Isenmann E, et al. Effects of Expertise on Muscle Activity during the Hang Power Clean and Hang Power Snatch Compared to Snatch and Clean Pulls - An Explorative Analysis. J Sports Sci Med. 2023;22(4):778-789. PMID 38045750.
- [consensus, verified] Overhead pressing derivatives depend on triple extension of hips, knees and ankles; the strict press on top follows standard OHP mechanics. — Soriano MA, Suchomel TJ, Comfort P. Weightlifting Overhead Pressing Derivatives: A Review of the Literature. Sports Med. 2019;49(6):867-885. PMID 30924081.
- [emg, verified] Standing barbell press: anterior deltoid prime mover, medial deltoid synergist, triceps 39% higher with barbell than dumbbells. — Saeterbakken AH, Fimland MS. Effects of body position and loading modality on muscle activity and strength in shoulder presses. J Strength Cond Res. 2013;27(7):1824-1831. PMID 23096062.

Notes: Abs 0.5 from current is dropped: rectus abdominis acts as an isometric brace in the clean (Geisler 2023 labels it a stabiliser). Forearms/grip not credited, consistent with the RDL and row anchors. Eleven rows is a lot; if the UI needs fewer, drop calves and chest first.

### Landmine (jammer) press

`landmine_jammer_press` · 2 exercises · confidence low, unverified

Angled press along a landmine bar from the shoulders, driven from an athletic squat with explosive hip/knee/ankle extension. The bar path is roughly 45-60 degrees from horizontal, between an incline bench press and an overhead press, so the clavicular pec shares the work with the anterior deltoid.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Prime mover: shoulder flexion to ~120-140 degrees along the bar's arc. |
| chest | 0.75 {chest_upper 0.6, chest_mid 0.35, chest_lower 0.05} | RAISED from 0.5 and re-split to the incline-press anchor's distribution: at 45-60 degrees the clavicular head has a large flexion moment arm and the front-press pec effect (Coratella 2022) is amplified by the more horizontal bar path (incline-bench EMG, Rodriguez-Ridao 2020). |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Elbow extension at 45-135 degrees of shoulder elevation: intermediate between the bench and overhead splits (Kholinne 2018). |
| delt_side | 0.25 | REDUCED from 0.5: the arm path is close to the sagittal plane with little abduction, so middle-deltoid demand is smaller than in a true overhead press. |
| quads | 0.5 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9} | Kept: the jammer starts from a real squat ('squat by flexing your hips... extend powerfully'), deeper than a push-press dip. |
| glutes | 0.5 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1} | ADDED: hip extension out of the squat is the main lower-body driver of the jammer; mirrors the squat anchor's glute credit scaled to half. |
| calves | 0.25 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3} | REDUCED from 0.5: terminal plantarflexion only. |

Exercises: Landmine Linear Jammer, Single-Arm Linear Jammer.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Single-Arm Linear Jammer**: delt_front 1; chest 0.75 {chest_upper 0.6, chest_mid 0.35, chest_lower 0.05}; triceps 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6}; delt_side 0.25; quads 0.5 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9}; glutes 0.5 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1}; calves 0.25 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3}; obliques 0.25 — Single-arm loading adds an anti-lateral-flexion/anti-rotation demand on the obliques (unilateral-press principle from Saeterbakken & Fimland 2012); obliques 0.25.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] Front-of-head pressing excites pectoralis major far more than behind-neck pressing; the more horizontal the press vector, the larger the pec share. — Coratella G, Tornatore G, Longo S, Esposito F, Ce E. Front vs Back and Barbell vs Machine Overhead Press: An Electromyographic Analysis and Implications For Resistance Training. Front Physiol. 2022;13:825880. PMID 35936912.
- [emg, likely] Across five bench inclinations the clavicular pec peaks around 30 degrees and anterior deltoid activation rises with inclination, placing a 45-60 degree press in a zone shared by upper pec and anterior deltoid. — Rodriguez-Ridao D, Antequera-Vique JA, Martin-Fuentes I, Muyor JM. Effect of Five Bench Inclinations on the Electromyographic Activity of the Pectoralis Major, Anterior Deltoid, and Triceps Brachii during the Bench Press Exercise. Int J Environ Res Public Health. 2020;17(19):7339. doi:10.3390/ijerph17197339.
- [emg, verified] Landmine loading shifts force posteriorly/horizontally and reduces quadriceps activity relative to a vertical goblet squat, so leg credit for the jammer squat is indirect (0.5), not primary. — Collins KS, Klawitter LA, Waldera RW, Mahoney SJ, Christensen BK. Differences in Muscle Activity and Kinetics Between the Goblet Squat and Landmine Squat in Men and Women. J Strength Cond Res. 2021;35(10):2661-2668. PMID 34341315.
- [emg, verified] Medial/lateral heads of triceps dominate at 90 degrees and above, long head at low shoulder elevation; the landmine arc spans both, hence the .4/.6 split. — Kholinne E, et al. The different role of each head of the triceps brachii muscle in elbow extension. Acta Orthop Traumatol Turc. 2018;52(3):201-205. PMID 29503079.

Notes: No direct landmine-press EMG exists in PubMed; the chest/deltoid split is anatomical, bracketed by the incline-press anchor and the front OHP. Hamstrings 0.5 and abs 0.5 from current are dropped (squat pattern: no hamstring credit per the squat anchor; abs are an isometric brace). Serratus anterior is heavily involved in the protracted finish and is not separately credited. Single-Arm_Linear_Jammer's current row lacked leg credit although the movement is the same squat-drive; it now inherits the family's leg rows.

### Handstand push-up

`handstand_push_up` · 1 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Inverted bodyweight vertical press: from a handstand (usually wall-supported) the head lowers to the floor and the body is pressed back to lockout. Mechanically an overhead press with ~85-100% bodyweight, elbows typically tracking forward of the wrists (tripod head position), with heavy demand on the upper trapezius for scapular elevation/stability.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | Prime mover; anterior deltoid is the dominant shoulder contributor in the handstand (Kochanowicz 2018) and the press adds its dynamic flexion role. |
| delt_side | 0.5 | Abduction synergist as in a front press (Campos 2020). |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Elbow extension against bodyweight; triceps was a top-four contributor in the handstand (13-16%) and works through full range in the push-up; medial-head bias overhead (Kholinne 2018). |
| traps_upper | 0.5 | Trapezius descendens was the single largest contributor (20-26%) in the static handstand (Kochanowicz 2018) and the press adds a loaded shrug at lockout. |
| chest | 0.25 {chest_upper 0.8, chest_mid 0.2} | Front-press clavicular pec contribution, larger when the head is placed forward of the hands (tripod), which angles the press toward an incline path. |

Exercises: Handstand Push-Ups.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] In a handstand, trapezius descendens (20-26%) and wrist flexors are the largest contributors, followed by anterior deltoid (15-18%) and triceps brachii (13-16%). — Kochanowicz A, Niespodzinski B, Marina M, Mieszkowski J, Biskup L, Kochanowicz K. Relationship between postural control and muscle activity during a handstand in young and adult gymnasts. Hum Mov Sci. 2018;58:195-204. PMID 29471194.
- [consensus, verified] Systematic review of handstand biomechanics: only ~10% of studies report EMG; no study measures the handstand push-up itself, so the press mapping is transferred from overhead-press EMG. — MacDonald M, Baker JS, Gu Y, Ugbolue UC. Biomechanical analyses of the handstand: a systematic review. Front Sports Act Living. 2025;7:1694648. PMID 41473027.
- [emg, verified] Anterior deltoid > medial deltoid >> posterior deltoid in the shoulder press. — Campos YAC, et al. Different Shoulder Exercises Affect the Activation of Deltoid Portions in Resistance-Trained Individuals. J Hum Kinet. 2020;75:5-14. PMID 33312291.

Notes: Forearm/wrist flexors were the most active muscles in the handstand EMG but that is postural balance work; not credited (consistent with no forearm credit elsewhere in pressing). Same rows as the bilateral press family; kept separate because it is bodyweight and the user expects it listed.

### Anti-gravity (prone incline) press-out

`prone_incline_press_out` · 1 exercises · confidence low, unverified

Lying face-down on an incline bench, the bar is reverse-curled to the chest then pressed forward past the head with the arms kept parallel to the floor. Gravity loads the shoulder in ~135-150 degrees of flexion throughout (anterior deltoid and scapular retractors/upward rotators hold the arms up) while the elbows extend to push the bar away.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| delt_front | 1 | The arms are held horizontal against gravity in deep flexion for the whole rep: the anterior deltoid carries the largest load moment (anatomical reasoning; free-exercise-db lists shoulders primary). |
| upper_back | 0.5 {traps_mid_lower 0.5, rhomboids 0.5} | Kept: prone with the arms out front, the middle/lower trapezius and rhomboids must fix and upwardly rotate the scapula against the load (prone-flexion exercises load lower/middle trapezius strongly, Andersen 2012). |
| triceps | 0.5 {triceps_long 0.4, triceps_lateral_medial 0.6} | Elbow extension is the described movement; load moment about the elbow rises as the forearm comes level. Shoulder elevated >90 degrees so medial-head bias (Kholinne 2018). |
| traps_upper | 0.25 | REDUCED from 0.5: upper trapezius assists elevation/upward rotation but in the prone position the load line favours the lower/middle fibres. |
| delt_side | 0.25 | REDUCED from 0.5: some abduction component with the arms out at shoulder width, but the position is predominantly sagittal-plane flexion. |

Exercises: Anti-Gravity Press.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] Prone flexion (arms raised in front while lying prone) predominantly activates the lower and middle trapezius over the upper trapezius. — Andersen CH, Zebis MK, Saervoll C, et al. Scapular muscle activity from selected strengthening exercises performed at low and high intensities. J Strength Cond Res. 2012;26(9):2408-2416. PMID 22076101.
- [emg, verified] Medial head of triceps dominates elbow extension at 135-180 degrees shoulder elevation. — Kholinne E, et al. The different role of each head of the triceps brachii muscle in elbow extension. Acta Orthop Traumatol Turc. 2018;52(3):201-205. PMID 29503079.
- [consensus, verified] Anterior deltoid is the primary shoulder flexor loaded when the arm is held forward against gravity (free-exercise-db lists shoulders primary; middle back, traps, triceps secondary). — yuhonas/free-exercise-db exercise record 'Anti-Gravity Press' (Unlicense), plus standard functional anatomy of deltoid.

Notes: Obscure exercise with no direct literature; mapping is anatomical. Kept close to current, trimming traps_upper and delt_side to 0.25.

## small groups

### Standing / knee-extended calf raise

`standing_calf_raise` · 10 exercises · confidence high, unverified

Ankle plantar flexion with the knee at or near full extension (standing, donkey, leg-press calf press, Smith, band, dumbbell). Gastrocnemius is at long length and contributes >=40% of torque; soleus is a co-primary plantar flexor.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| calves | 1 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3} | Matches the Standing_Calf_Raises anchor. Kinoshita 2023: 12 wk knee-extended calf raises grew medial/lateral gastrocnemius 9-12% vs soleus ~2%, so emphasis is strongly gastrocnemius even though soleus also grows. Current mapping is already right for every exercise here; kept. |

Exercises: Calf Press, Calf Press On The Leg Press Machine, Calf Raise On A Dumbbell, Calf Raises - With Bands, Donkey Calf Raises, Rocking Standing Calf Raise, Smith Machine Calf Raise, Standing Barbell Calf Raise, Standing Calf Raises, Standing Dumbbell Calf Raise.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Rocking Standing Calf Raise**: calves 1 {gastrocnemius 0.7, soleus 0.3}; tibialis 0.5 — The 'rocking' phase rolls back onto the heels and lifts the forefoot (loaded dorsiflexion) between calf raises, so tibialis anterior does concentric work each rep; secondary credit because the dorsiflexion load is only body + bar mass through a short arc.

Evidence:

- [hypertrophy, verified] Knee-extended (standing) calf-raise training produced 9.2% (medial) and 12.4% (lateral) gastrocnemius volume gains vs 0.6%/1.7% for knee-flexed; soleus grew similarly in both (2.1% vs 2.9%). — Kinoshita M, Maeo S, Kobayashi Y, et al. Triceps surae muscle hypertrophy is greater after standing versus seated calf-raise training. Front Physiol. 2023;14:1272106. PMID 38156065.
- [emg, verified] With the knee extended the gastrocnemius contributes at least 40% of plantar-flexor torque; its EMG falls with knee flexion while soleus EMG is unchanged. — Cresswell AG, Loscher WN, Thorstensson A. Influence of gastrocnemius muscle length on triceps surae torque development and electromyographic activity in man. Exp Brain Res. 1995;105(2):283-290. PMID 7498381.
- [hypertrophy, verified] Calf raises on a horizontal leg press (knee extended) increased medial and lateral gastrocnemius thickness 4-9% in 9 weeks; foot-out biased the medial head, foot-in the lateral head. — Nunes JP, Costa BDV, Kassiano W, et al. Different foot positioning during calf training to induce portion-specific gastrocnemius muscle hypertrophy. J Strength Cond Res. 2020;34(8):2347-2351. PMID 32735428.
- [hypertrophy, verified] Leg-press calf raises performed in the stretched (dorsiflexed) half of the ROM produced ~15% gastrocnemius thickness gains vs ~7% full ROM and ~3-6% shortened ROM, confirming long-length loading drives gastrocnemius growth. — Kassiano W, Costa B, Kunevaliki G, et al. Greater gastrocnemius muscle hypertrophy after partial range of motion training performed at long muscle lengths. J Strength Cond Res. 2023;37(9):1746-1753. PMID 37015016.
- [anatomical, likely] Donkey calf raises flex the hip, not the knee; gastrocnemius crosses the knee only, so its length and share are the same as a standing raise. — Neumann DA. Kinesiology of the Musculoskeletal System. 3rd ed. Elsevier; 2017 (ankle/foot chapter: triceps surae attachments and actions).

Notes: All ten exercises keep the anchor distribution. Calf_Press and Calf_Press_On_The_Leg_Press_Machine are performed with a near-straight knee (the studies above used exactly this setup), so they belong with standing rather than seated raises.

### Seated / knee-flexed calf raise

`seated_calf_raise` · 3 exercises · confidence high, unverified

Plantar flexion with the knee flexed ~90 deg, which slackens the gastrocnemius and shifts the work to the soleus.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| calves | 1 {gastrocnemius 0.2, soleus 0.8} | Seated_Calf_Raise anchor. Gastrocnemius is actively insufficient at 90 deg knee flexion (Cresswell 1995) and did not grow at all after 12 wk seated training (Kinoshita 2023). Current mapping already correct for all three; kept. |

Exercises: Barbell Seated Calf Raise, Dumbbell Seated One-Leg Calf Raise, Seated Calf Raise.

Evidence:

- [hypertrophy, verified] Knee-flexed calf-raise training produced no significant gastrocnemius growth (0.6-1.7%) but soleus growth (2.9%) equal to standing training. — Kinoshita M, Maeo S, Kobayashi Y, et al. Triceps surae muscle hypertrophy is greater after standing versus seated calf-raise training. Front Physiol. 2023;14:1272106. PMID 38156065.
- [emg, verified] Maximal plantar-flexor torque falls to ~60% at 60 deg knee flexion and gastrocnemius EMG decreases with knee flexion while soleus EMG is unchanged. — Cresswell AG, Loscher WN, Thorstensson A. Influence of gastrocnemius muscle length on triceps surae torque development and electromyographic activity in man. Exp Brain Res. 1995;105(2):283-290. PMID 7498381.

Notes: Barbell and dumbbell seated variants are mechanically identical to the machine; the one-leg dumbbell version is unilateral but that changes load, not emphasis.

### Tibialis (reverse calf) raise

`tibialis_raise` · 1 exercises · confidence high, unverified

Loaded ankle dorsiflexion: heels on the ground or a step, forefoot lifts against the load. Tibialis anterior is the prime mover; the triceps surae is unloaded.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| tibialis | 1 | A 'reverse calf raise' is dorsiflexion with the bar on the shoulders and heels on a block; the plantar flexors do no concentric work. The current calves 1.0 mapping is a naming error and should be replaced with tibialis 1.0 (calves 0). |

Exercises: Smith Machine Reverse Calf Raises.

Evidence:

- [emg, likely] Tibialis anterior EMG peak during resisted isometric dorsiflexion is closely tied to its thickness and pennation (R2 = 0.69), i.e. dorsiflexion loading directly targets this muscle. — Ruiz-Munoz M, Cuesta-Vargas AI. Electromyography and sonomyography analysis of the tibialis anterior: a cross sectional study. J Foot Ankle Res. 2014;7:11. doi:10.1186/1757-1146-7-11.
- [emg, verified] Progressive dorsiflexion resistance training (300 reps/day for 2 months) raised tibialis anterior active EMG RMS and dorsiflexion function versus standard rehab in a randomized trial. — Li Q, Chen L. Effect of tibialis anterior muscle resistance training on ankle and foot dorsum extension function in hypertensive cerebral hemorrhage hemiplegia patients: a randomized controlled trial. Medicine (Baltimore). 2023;102(31):e33827. PMID 37543805.
- [anatomical, likely] Tibialis anterior is the largest dorsiflexor (with extensor digitorum longus and extensor hallucis longus assisting); the triceps surae is the antagonist and is not loaded in dorsiflexion. — Neumann DA. Kinesiology of the Musculoskeletal System. 3rd ed. Elsevier; 2017 (ankle/foot chapter).

Notes: Tibialis is an opt-in group; if a user has it disabled the exercise will show no colour, which is correct (it should not light the calves).

### Shrug (scapular elevation)

`shrug` · 7 exercises · confidence high, unverified

Scapular elevation against a load held in the hands or on the shoulders: barbell, dumbbell, cable, Smith, leverage machine, calf-machine shrug, behind-the-back variants. Upper trapezius and levator scapulae elevate; middle trapezius and rhomboids assist elevation/retraction; grip is loaded isometrically in the handheld versions.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| traps_upper | 1 | Shrugs produce the highest upper-trapezius EMG of any exercise tested (Ekstrom 2003; ~100% MVC in Andersen 2008). Levator scapulae has no group of its own and is folded into traps_upper. |
| upper_back | 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.7, rhomboids 0.3} | Middle trapezius and rhomboids shorten during elevation and were highly active (>60% MVC) in shrug sets (Jakobsen 2012); minor credit so the mid-back shows colour without competing with rows. |
| forearms | 0.25 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} | Handheld shrugs are usually the heaviest load a lifter grips; finger-flexor EMG scales with grip demand (Krings 2021). Minor credit only because the work is isometric; the machine (shoulder-pad) shrug gets none. |

Exercises: Barbell Shrug, Barbell Shrug Behind The Back, Cable Shrugs, Calf-Machine Shoulder Shrug, Dumbbell Shrug, Leverage Shrug, Smith Machine Behind the Back Shrug.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Barbell Shrug Behind The Back**: traps_upper 1; upper_back 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.6, rhomboids 0.4}; forearms 0.25 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} — Same as the Smith behind-the-back: retraction bias. The current upper_back 0.5 and forearms 0.5 are higher than any other shrug for no measured reason; brought in line with the family (0.25 each).
- **Leverage Shrug**: traps_upper 1; upper_back 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.7, rhomboids 0.3}; forearms 0.25 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} — Current forearms 0.5 with a 50/50 split is out of line with the other handheld shrugs; grip is finger-flexor work, and a machine handle is no harder to hold than a barbell.
- **Smith Machine Behind the Back Shrug**: traps_upper 1; upper_back 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.6, rhomboids 0.4}; forearms 0.25 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} — Bar behind the body pulls the scapulae into slight retraction, so nudge the upper_back split toward rhomboids. The current delt_front 0.5 is unsupported: there is no shoulder flexion in a shrug; remove it.
- **Calf-Machine Shoulder Shrug**: traps_upper 1; upper_back 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.7, rhomboids 0.3} — Load sits on the shoulder pads, nothing is gripped, so no forearm credit.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] The unilateral shoulder shrug produced the greatest upper-trapezius EMG of ten trapezius/serratus exercises. — Ekstrom RA, Donatelli RA, Soderberg GL. Surface electromyographic analysis of exercises for the trapezius and serratus anterior muscles. J Orthop Sports Phys Ther. 2003;33(5):247-258. PMID 12774999.
- [emg, verified] Shrugs elicited 102 +/- 11% MVC trapezius activation, higher than lateral raise (97%) and upright row (85%), at 20-30 kg loads. — Andersen LL, Kjaer M, Andersen CH, et al. Muscle activation during selected strength exercises in women with chronic neck muscle pain. Phys Ther. 2008;88(6):703-711. PMID 18339796.
- [emg, verified] During 15RM shrug sets normalized EMG of upper, middle and lower trapezius was high (>60% MVC) and rose within each set. — Jakobsen MD, Sundstrup E, Andersen CH, Zebis MK, Mortensen P, Andersen LL. Evaluation of muscle activity during a standardized shoulder resistance training bout in novice individuals. J Strength Cond Res. 2012;26(9):2515-2522. PMID 22067242.
- [emg, verified] Shrugging with the arms abducted ~30 deg increases upper- and lower-trapezius activity versus the standard shrug, showing the whole trapezius participates in elevation. — Pizzari T, Wickham J, Balster S, Ganderton C, Watson L. Modifying a shrug exercise can facilitate the upward rotator muscles of the scapula. Clin Biomech (Bristol). 2014;29(2):201-205. PMID 24342452.
- [emg, verified] Increasing grip demand (fat grips) raised flexor/extensor carpi EMG during deadlift and bent-over row while lowering 1RM, i.e. forearm activity in heavy holds is real but load-limiting rather than hypertrophy-optimal. — Krings BM, Shepherd BD, Swain JC, et al. Impact of fat grip attachments on muscular strength and neuromuscular activation during resistance exercise. J Strength Cond Res. 2021;35(2S):S152-S157. PMID 30694963.

Notes: No study directly compares behind-the-back with front shrugs; the retraction nudge is anatomical only. Levator scapulae is credited to traps_upper because it has no group of its own.

### Sumo high pull

`high_pull` · 1 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Explosive wide-stance hip/knee extension flowing into a narrow-grip upright-row: the bell is pulled to chest height by scapular elevation and shoulder abduction with elbows leading.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| traps_upper | 1 | The finish is a shrug plus upright row; upper trapezius shows 85%+ MVC in upright rows (Andersen 2008) and is the dominant elevator. |
| delt_side | 0.5 | Elbows-high abduction loads the lateral deltoid; with a single-handle (narrow) grip deltoid involvement is lower than a wide-grip upright row (McAllister 2013), so 0.5 not 1.0. |
| delt_rear | 0.25 | Posterior deltoid activity in the upright row rises with grip width; a narrow kettlebell grip keeps it minor. |
| biceps | 0.25 {biceps 0.5, brachialis 0.5} | Narrow grip increases biceps involvement in the upright row (McAllister 2013); pronated grip biases brachialis/brachioradialis. |
| glutes | 0.5 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1} | Each rep starts from a sumo hinge/squat and drives through hip extension (kettlebell hinge patterns show very high hip-extensor activity, Zebis 2013). |
| hamstrings | 0.25 {hamstrings_hip_ext 1} | Hip-extension contribution in a shortened hinge; short head is not involved. |
| quads | 0.25 {quads_rf 0.1, quads_vasti 0.9} | Knee extension from a partial sumo squat with a light bell; minor. |
| adductors | 0.25 | Wide sumo stance recruits adductor magnus as a hip extensor (Escamilla 2002: adductor activity greater at higher knee flexion in deadlifts); minor because the bell is light. |

Exercises: Kettlebell Sumo High Pull.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] In the upright row, lateral and posterior deltoid and trapezius activity increase with grip width while biceps activity decreases; narrow grips shift work toward the biceps. — McAllister MJ, Schilling BK, Hammond KG, Weiss LW, Farney TM. Effect of grip width on electromyographic activity during the upright row. J Strength Cond Res. 2013;27(1):181-187. PMID 22362088.
- [emg, verified] Upright rows produced 85% MVC trapezius activation. — Andersen LL, Kjaer M, Andersen CH, et al. Muscle activation during selected strength exercises in women with chronic neck muscle pain. Phys Ther. 2008;88(6):703-711. PMID 18339796.
- [emg, verified] Kettlebell hinge/swing patterns load the hamstrings (semitendinosus 73-115% MVC) and hip extensors at very high levels. — Zebis MK, Skotte J, Andersen CH, et al. Kettlebell swing targets semitendinosus and supine leg curl targets biceps femoris: an EMG study with rehabilitation implications. Br J Sports Med. 2013;47(18):1192-1198. PMID 22736206.
- [emg, verified] In deadlift variants hip adductor and gluteus maximus EMG were greater in the more knee-flexed intervals, supporting adductor credit for wide-stance pulls. — Escamilla RF, Francisco AC, Kayes AV, Speer KP, Moorman CT. An electromyographic analysis of sumo and conventional style deadlifts. Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2002;34(4):682-688. PMID 11932579.

Notes: Versus current: delt_rear 0.5 -> 0.25, hamstrings/quads/adductors 0.5 -> 0.25, glutes kept 0.5, biceps 0.25 added. The kettlebell load is small relative to the legs, so lower-body credit is minor stabiliser/synergist credit rather than half.

### Rack pull (partial deadlift from knee height)

`rack_pull` · 2 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Top-half deadlift from pins at or just below the knee: near-isometric spinal erector bracing under very heavy load with hip extension to lockout; little knee extension. Upper traps and grip are loaded heavily at lockout.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| erectors | 1 | Erector spinae is among the most active muscles in deadlift variants and its activation scales with external torque (Martin-Fuentes 2020; Moreira 2023); rack pulls allow supra-maximal loads so ES demand stays highest. Current 1.0 kept. |
| glutes | 0.5 {glute_max 0.9, glute_med 0.1} | Hip extension from ~knee height to lockout; glute max share raised to 0.9 to match the squat/RDL anchors (current 0.8/0.2). |
| hamstrings | 0.5 {hamstrings_hip_ext 1} | Biceps femoris/gastrocnemius activity is greatest in the mid-pull/lockout portion (Moreira 2023). Knee is nearly straight so only the hip-extending heads work; current 0.8/0.2 split changed to 1.0/0 to match the Romanian_Deadlift anchor. |
| traps_upper | 0.5 | Upper trapezius EMG is greatest in the low-knee-flexion (lockout) intervals of a deadlift (Escamilla 2002), which is the whole range of a rack pull. Kept at 0.5. |
| upper_back | 0.25 {traps_mid_lower 0.6, rhomboids 0.4} | Middle trapezius is active throughout the deadlift (Escamilla 2002) holding the scapulae against very heavy loads; minor credit. |
| forearms | 0.5 {forearm_flexors 0.8, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.2} | Rack pulls are typically the heaviest thing a lifter holds; finger-flexor EMG tracks grip demand (Krings 2021). Split changed from 50/50 to flexor-biased because gripping is finger flexion; extensors only stabilise the wrist. |

Exercises: Rack Pull with Bands, Rack Pulls.

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] Across deadlift variants erector spinae and quadriceps show greater activation than gluteus maximus and biceps femoris; semitendinosus slightly exceeds biceps femoris. — Martin-Fuentes I, Oliva-Lozano JM, Muyor JM. Electromyographic activity in deadlift exercise and its variants. A systematic review. PLoS One. 2020;15(2):e0229507. PMID 32107499.
- [emg, verified] In isometric pulls at lift-off, mid-pull and lockout, rectus femoris and erector spinae were highest at lift-off, biceps femoris and lateral gastrocnemius highest at mid-pull, and erector spinae activation increased with external torque. — Moreira VM, Lima LCR, Mortatti AL, et al. Analysis of muscle strength and electromyographic activity during different deadlift positions. Muscles. 2023;2(2):218-227. PMID 40757569.
- [emg, verified] Quadriceps, hip adductor, gluteus maximus, paraspinal and middle trapezius EMG were greater in higher knee-flexion intervals, whereas hamstring, gastrocnemius and upper trapezius EMG were greater in lower knee-flexion (lockout) intervals. — Escamilla RF, Francisco AC, Kayes AV, Speer KP, Moorman CT. An electromyographic analysis of sumo and conventional style deadlifts. Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2002;34(4):682-688. PMID 11932579.
- [emg, verified] Forearm flexor/extensor EMG in the deadlift rises with grip difficulty. — Krings BM, Shepherd BD, Swain JC, et al. Impact of fat grip attachments on muscular strength and neuromuscular activation during resistance exercise. J Strength Cond Res. 2021;35(2S):S152-S157. PMID 30694963.

Notes: Quads removed for both variants (Rack_Pull_with_Bands currently has quads 0.5, Rack_Pulls none): from knee height the knee-extension moment is small and quadriceps EMG concentrates at lift-off. Lats are not credited: they act isometrically to keep the bar close. No rack-pull-specific EMG exists; mapping is interpolated from deadlift position studies.

### Neck flexion / extension / lateral flexion

`neck_flexion_extension` · 5 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Direct cervical loading: weighted or manually-resisted head extension (splenius, semispinalis, upper cervical erectors), head flexion (sternocleidomastoid, longus colli/capitis, scalenes) and side-bending (both groups). Only these direct exercises hypertrophy the neck; heavy compound lifts do not.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| neck | 1 {neck_flexors 0.5, neck_extensors 0.5} | Family default for the two isometric exercises that load both directions in one set; overrides below assign single-direction exercises 100% to one region. Current mappings are already correct for all five; kept. |

Exercises: Isometric Neck Exercise - Front And Back, Isometric Neck Exercise - Sides, Lying Face Down Plate Neck Resistance, Lying Face Up Plate Neck Resistance, Seated Head Harness Neck Resistance.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Isometric Neck Exercise - Sides**: neck 1 {neck_flexors 0.5, neck_extensors 0.5} — Lateral flexion is produced by ipsilateral SCM and scalenes (flexor bucket) together with splenius, levator scapulae and upper cervical erectors (extensor bucket); equal split.
- **Lying Face Down Plate Neck Resistance**: neck 1 {neck_extensors 1} — Prone plate-resisted head extension: Conley 1997 showed 24-25% CSA gains in splenius capitis and semispinalis capitis/cervicis from exactly this movement.
- **Lying Face Up Plate Neck Resistance**: neck 1 {neck_flexors 1} — Supine plate-resisted head flexion: sternocleidomastoid CSA is the morphological correlate of flexor strength and endurance (Rozenstoka 2026); extensors are unloaded.
- **Seated Head Harness Neck Resistance**: neck 1 {neck_extensors 1} — Head harness with the plate hanging in front resists extension (the standard use in free-exercise-db); same movers as the prone plate extension.

Evidence:

- [hypertrophy, verified] 12 weeks of 3x10 head-extension exercise increased total neck CSA (19.5 -> 22.0 cm2), driven by ~24% CSA gains in splenius capitis, semispinalis capitis and semispinalis cervicis; heavy compound lifting alone (squat, deadlift, push press, row, mid-thigh pull) produced no neck hypertrophy. — Conley MS, Stone MH, Nimmons M, Dudley GA. Specificity of resistance training responses in neck muscle size and strength. Eur J Appl Physiol Occup Physiol. 1997;75(5):443-448. PMID 9189733.
- [emg, verified] MRI T2-shift mapping showed head extension recruits splenius capitis, semispinalis capitis/cervicis and multifidus (60% of their CSA pre-training), with levator scapulae, longissimus and scalenes contributing before training. — Conley MS, Stone MH, Nimmons M, Dudley GA. Resistance training and human cervical muscle recruitment plasticity. J Appl Physiol (1985). 1997;83(6):2105-2111. PMID 9390988.
- [anatomical, verified] Sternocleidomastoid CSA is independently associated with neck flexor strength (beta = 1.32 kg per cm2) and flexor endurance but not extensor strength, confirming SCM as the flexion prime mover. — Rozenstoka S, Comadran de Barnola E, Kauzena S, Akuratere G, Galeja Z, Deicmane D. Sternocleidomastoid muscle morphology is independently associated with neck strength and endurance in athletes and para-athletes. Front Physiol. 2026;17:1852707. PMID 42661709.

Notes: Conley's finding that isometric neck bracing during heavy compounds caused no hypertrophy supports the brief's rule of not crediting isometric stabilisers; the two Isometric_Neck exercises are still credited at 1.0 because the neck is the direct target and manual resistance can be near-maximal. Upper trapezius is not credited for extension: it did not appear among the hypertrophying muscles.

### Wrist curl (palms-up) and finger curl

`wrist_curl` · 9 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Concentric wrist flexion (and, for finger curls, finger flexion) with a supinated forearm: flexor carpi radialis/ulnaris, palmaris longus and flexor digitorum superficialis/profundus. Wrist extensors are antagonists and nearly silent.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| forearms | 1 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} | Only the anterior-compartment flexors shorten under load; the extensor/brachioradialis region gets a token 0.1 for wrist-stabilising co-contraction. Current 0.8/0.2 is close; sharpened to 0.9/0.1 so that palms-up and palms-down curls are visibly distinct on the map. |

Exercises: Cable Wrist Curl, Finger Curls, Palms-Up Barbell Wrist Curl Over A Bench, Palms-Up Dumbbell Wrist Curl Over A Bench, Seated Dumbbell Palms-Up Wrist Curl, Seated One-Arm Dumbbell Palms-Up Wrist Curl, Seated Palm-Up Barbell Wrist Curl, Seated Two-Arm Palms-Up Low-Pulley Wrist Curl, Standing Palms-Up Barbell Behind The Back Wrist Curl.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Finger Curls**: forearms 1 {forearm_flexors 0.9, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.1} — Current 0.5/0.5 is wrong: letting the bar roll to the fingertips and curling it back is pure flexor digitorum superficialis/profundus work; extensors do nothing concentric.

Evidence:

- [anatomical, likely] Flexor carpi radialis and palmaris longus are the primary wrist flexors and flexor digitorum superficialis/profundus the primary finger flexors; all lie in the anterior (flexor) compartment, while the wrist extensors and brachioradialis are posterior/lateral and are antagonists to wrist flexion. — Neumann DA. Kinesiology of the Musculoskeletal System. 3rd ed. Elsevier; 2017 (wrist and hand chapters).
- [emg, verified] Flexor carpi radialis, palmaris longus and other anterior-compartment muscles show substantial activity in gripping/pronating efforts; wrist extensors contribute mainly stabilising co-activation. — Bader J, Boland MR, Greybe D, Nitz A, Uhl T, Pienkowski D. Muscle activity during maximal isometric forearm rotation using a power grip. J Biomech. 2018;68:24-32. PMID 29305049.
- [emg, verified] Forearm flexor and extensor EMG increase when grip demand rises (fat grip vs standard bar) during pulling exercises, showing forearm loading is grip-driven. — Krings BM, Shepherd BD, Swain JC, et al. Impact of fat grip attachments on muscular strength and neuromuscular activation during resistance exercise. J Strength Cond Res. 2021;35(2S):S152-S157. PMID 30694963.

Notes: No longitudinal study of wrist-curl hypertrophy was found; the mapping rests on anatomy and grip/forearm EMG. The behind-the-back standing version changes wrist position slightly but not the movers. Unilateral dumbbell versions change load, not emphasis.

### Reverse (palms-down) wrist curl

`reverse_wrist_curl` · 5 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Concentric wrist extension with a pronated forearm: extensor carpi radialis longus/brevis, extensor carpi ulnaris and extensor digitorum, with the finger flexors gripping the implement isometrically throughout.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| forearms | 1 {forearm_flexors 0.2, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.8} | Extensors do the concentric/eccentric work; flexors keep 0.2 because the bar must be gripped against gravity the whole set (finger-flexor isometric loading), unlike the palms-up curl where the bar rests in the fingers. Current mapping already correct; kept. |

Exercises: Palms-Down Dumbbell Wrist Curl Over A Bench, Palms-Down Wrist Curl Over A Bench, Seated Dumbbell Palms-Down Wrist Curl, Seated One-Arm Dumbbell Palms-Down Wrist Curl, Seated Palms-Down Barbell Wrist Curl.

Evidence:

- [anatomical, likely] Extensor carpi radialis longus/brevis and extensor carpi ulnaris are the prime wrist extensors; extensor digitorum assists. They occupy the posterior compartment together with brachioradialis, which is grouped with them in this taxonomy. — Neumann DA. Kinesiology of the Musculoskeletal System. 3rd ed. Elsevier; 2017 (wrist chapter).
- [emg, verified] Wrist extensors (ECR, ECU) and flexors (FCR, FCU) co-activate during dynamic wrist extension, with ECR/ECU dominant. — Eraktas I, Ayhan C, Hayran M, Soylu AR. Alterations in forearm muscle activation patterns after scapholunate interosseous ligament injury: a dynamic electromyography study. J Hand Ther. 2021;34(3):384-395. PMID 32620427.
- [emg, verified] Brachioradialis is primarily an elbow flexor (most active in neutral/supinated flexion), not a wrist extensor; its share of this region's credit in a reverse wrist curl is therefore incidental. — Caufriez B, Dugailly PM, Brassinne E, Schuind F. The role of the muscle brachioradialis in elbow flexion: an electromyographic study. J Hand Surg Asian Pac Vol. 2018;23(1):102-110. PMID 29409427.

Notes: The taxonomy lumps brachioradialis with the extensors; reverse wrist curls light that region even though brachioradialis itself is barely working. Reverse curls (elbow flexion, batch elsewhere) are the exercise that actually trains brachioradialis.

### Forearm pronation / supination

`forearm_rotation` · 3 exercises · confidence medium, unverified

Resisted rotation of the radius about the ulna with the elbow flexed. Pronation: pronator teres and pronator quadratus (anterior compartment) with brachioradialis, FCR and palmaris longus assisting. Supination: supinator (posterior compartment) and biceps brachii, with brachioradialis assisting from the pronated position.

| Group | Credit | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| forearms | 1 {forearm_flexors 0.5, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.5} | Family default for the bar rotation that alternates both directions; pronation is anterior-compartment work, supination is posterior-compartment (supinator) plus brachioradialis, so the two directions together split evenly. Current 0.5/0.5 for the bar rotation kept. |
| biceps | 0.25 {biceps 1} | Biceps brachii is the strongest supinator (52% MVC in maximal supination vs 5% in pronation, Bader 2018); credited for the supination half of the bar rotation only. |

Exercises: Dumbbell Lying Pronation, Dumbbell Lying Supination, Wrist Rotations with Straight Bar.

Per-exercise differences:

- **Dumbbell Lying Pronation**: forearms 1 {forearm_flexors 0.7, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.3} — Pronator teres, pronator quadratus, FCR and palmaris longus (all anterior/flexor compartment) drive pronation; brachioradialis is also significantly more active when pronating (Bader 2018), hence 0.3 to the extensor/brachioradialis region. Current 0.5/0.5 changed. No biceps credit: biceps EMG is ~5% MVC in pronation.
- **Dumbbell Lying Supination**: forearms 1 {forearm_flexors 0.3, forearm_extensors_brachioradialis 0.7}; biceps 0.5 {biceps 1} — Supinator sits in the posterior compartment and brachioradialis helps return from pronation, so the extensor/brachioradialis region takes the larger share; biceps brachii is the dominant high-torque supinator (Bader 2018; Kondi 2026) and gets half credit with all of it on the biceps region (brachialis does not supinate).

Evidence:

- [emg, verified] Biceps brachii was the only muscle active in one direction only: 52.1% MVC supinating vs 5.1% pronating. Brachioradialis, FCR, palmaris longus, pronator quadratus and pronator teres were significantly more active pronating; abductor pollicis longus and biceps more active supinating. — Bader J, Boland MR, Greybe D, Nitz A, Uhl T, Pienkowski D. Muscle activity during maximal isometric forearm rotation using a power grip. J Biomech. 2018;68:24-32. PMID 29305049.
- [emg, verified] Supinator and pronator quadratus dominate low-torque rotation; as torque rises biceps brachii (supination) and pronator teres (pronation) take over, i.e. loaded dumbbell rotations recruit biceps and pronator teres. — Kondi S, Murugesan T, Postans N, et al. An electromyographic study comparing muscle function during supination and pronation of the forearm. Cureus. 2026;18(1):e101255. PMID 41674760.
- [emg, verified] Brachioradialis is highly active in resisted elbow flexion in all forearm positions and acts as a rotator toward neutral, so it participates in both pronation from supination and supination from pronation. — Caufriez B, Dugailly PM, Brassinne E, Schuind F. The role of the muscle brachioradialis in elbow flexion: an electromyographic study. J Hand Surg Asian Pac Vol. 2018;23(1):102-110. PMID 29409427.

Notes: Pronator teres/quadratus and supinator have no region of their own; they are assigned to the flexor and extensor regions by compartment. Loads in these exercises are light, so absolute stimulus is small, but the credit shows the user which side of the forearm is being worked.
